STALEMATE 
IN SOUTH 


Hankow, Yesterday. 

Although fighting north of the 
Tientsin-Pukow Railway ig. con- 
tinuing with undiminished. vio- 
lence, it appears from bulletins 
issued by both sides that neither 
has gained any appreciable ad- 
vantage. 

Neither Chowhasien nor Tsining 
have been captured by the 
Chinese, who, however, have pe- 
hetrated the outskirts of both ci- 
ties. 

There seems to be little change 
on the front south of the T.P.R. 

The truth appears to be ‘that 
both the Chinese and Japanese 
have scored successes and suffer- 
ed reverses to the extent that 
the positions have remained vir- 
tually unaltered in the past few 
days,—Trans-Ocean,

IN TOKYO © 
ANTI-WAR. 
(CLASH | 


Shanghai, Yesterday. 

Private reports from 
Tokyo 'reccived this after- 
noon state that over 30 
persons were killed and 
about 1,000 arrested yester- 
day neor Tokyo when an 
anti-war demonstration by 
rver 3,000 persons took 
place. 

According to the report, 
which is credited to private in- 
formation received by 2 foreign 
concern in Shanghai, mothers, 
wives and sisters of 2,000 Jap- 
anese troops which were about 
to depart for China were respon- 
sible for the demonstration. 

The women marched through 
the streets ond assembled at the 
wharf where the troops were 
embarking. Shouting anti-war 
slogans the women boarded the 
transports and attempted to 
drag their relatives back to the 
wharf, 

Some of the troops joined in the 
demonstration and the Police were 
summoned te the scene. A clash 
with the demonstrators occurred 
resulting in over 80 being killed. 
—~Our ‘Own Correspondent.

pioncer among women. 
- There ure those who break 
new ground by reason of op- 
portunity and their own strong 
individuality, and there are 
thone who, fired by broader iden- 
linm and by impatience with the

round them, fight on behalf of 
Rumanity until they have made

and prejudice.:

104, Gd), belongs to the first 
type, but she does not qualify 
for inclusion in the second. 

She cntls her book, rather 
coyly, “The Autobiography of a 
Cook.” .

very much -more into her 
seventy-four vital, interesting 
yenra besides cooking.

major iJls, the unpleasant side 
of Hte shut firmly outside its

able than any life has been be- 
fore or since.

+ One of the most practical and 
sensible contributions to the 
traffic safety problem 1 have 
‘come across is included in this 
month's “Safety News." 

Up till now there has been 1 
tendency to tench children cau. 
tion by means of negatives and 
taboos. It is the fact thac 
these are inevitably broken in 
the first thoughtless moment of 
excitement or wilfulness that 
leads to the heavy toll of chitd 
life. 

But the writer of this article 
points out that the only pro- 
gressive means of safeguarding 
children against traffic dangers 
ix by playing on the inherent 
mechanical enthusiasm — which 
ig part of the equipment of the 
new and strangely technically 


minded generation. 

He suggests lessons on “How 
Trafic Lights Work,” although 
possibly quite a few modern 
children might be more compet- 
ent to instruct their parents on 
xuch matters than vice versa. 

Another idea is to utilise that 
extraordinary veneration for 
speed and speed — kings, by 
stressing the precautions which 
have to be taken in’ record 
breaking.

PARLIAMENT 
CONVENED 


Prague, Yesterday. 
The Czecho-Slovakian Senate 
and Chamber have been convok- 
ed for Tuesday and Wednesday. 
Political circles anticipate that 


the Government will make & de- 
claration dealings with the situa- 
tion at home and abroad, with 
particular reference to Austrinn 
affairs and. Herr Hitler’s speech 
to the Reichstag on Sunday. 

The Premier, Dr. Hodza, and 
the Foreign Minister, Dr. Krofta, 
discussed the situation — to-day 
with the French and  Yugo-Sla- 
vian Ministers in Prague, but 
semi-officially it is stated = ‘that 
“there is no reason for pessimism 
as far us Czecho-Slovakia is con- 
cerned.—Trans-Ocean, 


ZEISS-INQUART. 
RETURNS HOME 


Berlin, Yesterday. 

Herr Zeiss-Inquart, the new 
Austrian Minister of the Interior, 
who has been visiting Berlin, has 
had - talks with  Field-Marshal 
Hermann Goering, Rudolf Hess 
(Herr Hitler’s Deputy) and other 
prominent Nazi leaders. 

He lunched yesterday. with Dr. 
Wilhelm Frick, the German Minis- 
ter of the Interior. 

Herr Zeiss-Inquart, left Berlin 
for Vienna yesterday evening.—

by the Chinese 
eleven Japanese 


aviation authorities that 
machines 


(ten pursuit 


planes and one heavy bomber) were shot 
down, and not five, as was at first claimed.

have been three planes destroy- 
ed and one damagred. 

Two Chinese pilots were kil!- 
ed, one of them beings Squadron- 
Leader Li Kwei-tang, the Chin- 
ose “ace,” while another pilot 
was injured,

the wreckage of Japanese planes, 
and of two Japanese pilots who 
were captured alive.

CHINESE “ACE" KILLED 

Of the four Chinese planes downed

CARGO

ure reticent regarding the

which arrived. in Hong’ '

up a cargo of pig-iron con- 
signed to Japan, was com- 
pelled to sail on Friday 
morning with less than half 
the cargo aboard her.

of the ship was proceeding when 
members of-a Chinese patriotic or- 
ganisation intervened and caused 
the Chinese dock coolies to ecnse 
work. 

Police intervened but the “ngrita- 
tors” had by that tlme dissppeared 
while the wharf coolies had decid- 
a to decline to continue the load- 
ng.

KB.G.C. BIDS 
FAREWELL 10 
FL. RAPLEY 


The popularity and esteem 
with which Mr. I’, L. Rapley is 
held by the members of the 
Kowloon Bowling Green Club 
was fully demonstrated at a din- 
ner held in his honour at the 
Clubhouse last night and pre 
sided over by Mr. FE. V. Searle, 
the vice-president, 

Mr. Rapley, who has been 
closely associated with the Club 
for many years and who has 
served as president, is leaving 
for Home shortly on retirement. 
Last night he was presented 
with two Tientsin rugs by mem- 
bers of the Club. 

Mr. E. V, Searle, vice-president 
of the club, said: Our old friend 
Fred Rapley has decided to go 
Home and stay there and will gail 
on March 6th, by “Magara.” Well, 
I don'’t:on this occasion intend to 
pour out a lot of 
slush to the embarragsment of all, 
and to the detriment of my diges- 
tion. Fred has been one of the best 
and most conscientious officers 
the Club has ever had; he has 
never shirked a job of work, and 
has held practically every office 
including that of president, Pretty 
kindly sort of “bloke” too. 1 
served my bowls apprenticeship in 
his rink back in 1931 and he never 
made faces at me or mumbling 
mutterings, which many other 
skips have since. My recollections 
of Fred will always be that of x 
xood friend and sport and I'm 
sure that is how we all feel. 

On behalf of’ the members of 
this Club f will ask you, Fred, to 
accept this present, with our best 
wishes forthe good health and 
happiness of. yourself und your 
family, and long) may your lum 
rick, ‘ 

The “ovening’s enjoyment was. cn- 
livened by some two-minute lightning 
debates und an impromptu concert to 
which Messrs. C. B. Hosking, M. Rac- 
pave, 5 M.. White, J. G, Moyor,.and 


unoan contributed.

There he produced a suitcase. 


‘In it there were no stolen goods 


but there was-.o knife. With it 
Kiroo struck down the policeman, 
and tried to kill himself, but from 
the wound he recovered. ~ 


Then, after the shocking verdict 
of the lower Court, he got his 
chunce of a fair trial before tho 
Chief Justice. 


Luckily for him, he had an out- 
spoken British witness, The tor- 
ture had‘ been inflicted in sight 
of the “married lines” of the Bri- 
tish barracks. Some of the wives 
and children of the soldiers had 
seen It, and Pipe-Sergeant Ste- 
wart gave his evidence fearlessly. 


this treatment with passive cour- ° 


age; the weakling bribed and lied. 


Once travelling (as few Euro- 
peans do) in a local bus, I saw 
the driver “tip a policeman. It 
had to be done every day, he said, 
to keep them in a good temper. 


Again, travelling with Indians 
ino hired car, I was stopped by a 
sub-inspector, who alleged some 
wholly imaginary infraction of an 
unimportant by-law. He did not at 
first aee my white skin, and bullied 
my companions in the most brutal 
language—-whether for tho plea- 
sure of the thing or to extort a 
bribe, I do not know. When he saw 
me he saluted and apologised 
with sickening humility.

object, “is n tale of long ago. 
len’t thero full provincial aus 
tonomy to-day? Let the Indian 
Ministers put things straight.”

the power to do it, The constitu- 
tional position, after the Dichards 
at Westminster had done thelr 
worst, [s a compromise. 


it is true that for “law and or- 
der" Indian Ministers ate normin- 
ally responaible, But any scheme 
that they may draft for the reor- 
ganisation or discipline of the pro- 
vincial police force must receive 
the approval of the Governor,

:linelusion within the Reich for all practical purposes, are not fa 


ed in the Treaty of Versailles, but considering the general. at- 
titude of these same journals since the war, strongly re-aflirmed 
little more than three years ago after the Stresa Conference, if. 
becomes a point of intriguing interest as to whether an air of 
benevolent approval of the latest Hitler coup is possible without 
a feeling of acute embarrassment. : ; 
Almost equally interesting were Mr. Eden’s responses to the 
demand of the House of Commons for information on the devclop- 
ment. The Foreign Secretary made two things plain: that the 
British Government do not like the course events have taken at 
all; and that as long as Italy is prepared to accept the situation, 
the British Government does not propose to do anything about 
it. At the same time, Mr. Eden did not hesitate to present an 
opportunity for a test of the strength of the Berlin-Rome axis. 
Hitler's accomplishment of his greatest ambition is generally 
interpreted as the price required of Signor Mussolini for: main- 
tenance of the Rome-Berlin axis. Mr. Eden’s announcement 
that the British -Government were still prepared for the con- 
sultations contemplated in the Stresa Declaration may, by the 


same logic, be interpreted as a hint that if, on reflection, I! Duce 
is not altogether pleased with his bargain, no harm would come 
of talking things over. Not that the Foreign Secretary could

dreams.

grandmother and then danced a 
can-cun; but even so mad a pic- 
ture was hiding something of value 
to our lives. As Freud said: The 
dream never deals with trifles. 

We cannot approach the study 
of dreams until. we postulate that 
there is a ‘part of our personality 
that is hidden from us. This we 
call the Unconscious Mind, although

wide of the mark. It may also be assumed that the military 
demonstration along the border was -designed rather to assist 
Dr. Schuschnigg to persuade hia colleagues of the necessity of 
rendering unto Hiller that’ which was Cacsar’s, than it was a

RAW MILK CAN EVER BE REGARDED AS COM- 
PLETELY SAFE FOR HUMAN CONSUMPTION. 
Even if produced from healthy animals, milk is so sub- 
ject to contamination from human and other sources 
that it must always be regarded in the raw state as a 
potentially dangerous article of food. 


The only satisfactory method of ensuring final 
safety of the product is to submit it to some form of. 
heat treatment that can be relied on to destroy any 
pathogenic organisms that may be present.

we could pass the information on 
to you,

women may know what to wear, 
but quite half of them don't know 
how to wear it. 

We only saw two or three girls 
who really knew how to wear 
vlamorous frocks as well as how 
to choose them. IT asked Silvaine 
tu make a quick sketch of one of 
them while the girl wasn't logk- 
ing—and there it ia, She gets ten 
out of ten for glamour chic and 
individuality—and PH tell you 
why. 

. . °

every single detail, It was in that 
deep purplish) blue Ike corn- 
flowers In the sun. It had a high 
moulded corselette bodice, clever- 
ly draped, 

There was as good as nothing 
of it on top with the new halter 
top coming forward across the 
throat. And from the tight lttle 
waist a wide billowy skirt floated 
over a tight slip of satin, . 

Her shoes were sandals, edged 
with silver kid, her stockings were 
a pinky brown,

dingle flat flower poised to ono

Finally we liked her because

Eo wish we could say as much

1, This one was looped loving-

cheek to cheek. It's an inelegant 
position anyway, and besides, he 
mayn’t have felt that way about 
her, But apart from that, sho was 
wearing a brooch that seratched 
his shirtfront to threads, and in

vot a little ca ss about her

slipped down on to her aym, * 

- REMEDY: 

The front of a dress is a bad 
plaice for decorations—however 
distantly you dance, they're 
bound to get crushed, so wear 
them at the waist (right side), 
shoulder, or at the back. Second- 
ly lingerie atraps made of flesh 
net are almost invisible, and 
should be anchored with little 
slots on the shoulder of your 
frock, 

* ° . 

2. She understood the feminine 
allure of flowers all right—and 
used a bucketful, Flowers on her 
hair, on her frock, on her wrists, 
and [ don’t know where not. It 
Was as overpowering as a green- 
house, 

REMEDY: 

Two lots of flowers are quite 
enough and usually one is (the 
same rule applies to colour con- 
trasts). You can make a success 
of the flow on the hem if you. 
have only a single one in your 


chair and no more. Choose those 


to suit your personality. 
: x * & 

3. She had a skirt ihat was a 
tiny. bit too long to dance in, No 
doubt she'd heard that in mam- 
ma’s day they held up their trains 
by the hem...so she held hers 
that way, oblivious of the fact 
that she revealed most of her leg. 

REMEDY: 

Quite right, my dear, they did 
hold it that way, but mamma's 
frocks had yards of frills and she 
didn't show so much as an ankle. 
So either have your dance frock 
the new ankle length, or-hold it 
from the centre back about half 
way down the skirt, and drape it 
a little to one side.

cume obvious that she needed an 
underslip, Moreover, sho’d over-

IN DISGRACE WITH 
AUTHORITIES 
IN MOSCOW 


5 3russels, Yesterday. 
Another Soviet diplomatic official, this time one 
living in’ retirement, had a narrow escape 
_~when, according to a sensational report in a 
local paper, an attempt was made to assas- 
ginate Vice-Admiral Alexander Soboteff, 
former Soviet naval attache in Constantino- 

ple and Stockholm. 

He is said to have fallen in disgrace with the Mos- 
cow authorities in 1930, when he was attach- 
ed to the Soviet Legation in Stockholm, and 
was sentenced to death in absentia by a Mos- 
cow tribunal.

Police Intervene In 
Tokyo Political 
Gesture 


Tokyo, Yesterday. 
Police were compelled to intervene in a large 
scale political demonstration in Tokyo yester- 
day. : 
The demonstration was held in favour of dissolu- 
tion ‘of altpolitical parties and formation of 
a “front of national unity.” 


A large group, styling themselves the “defence corps against 
communism.” and dressed in khaki uniforms, marched to 
the headquarters of the two largest political parties, the Min- 
seito and © Selyukai,. carrying placards with inscriptions 
such xs “Dissolve the Corrupt Political Parties,” “This is 
Mussolini’s March on Rome.” 


When the demonstracors were declared they were entitled to en- 
ignored by party headquarters) ter ihe party buildings at will. 
they invaded the buildings and 
vecupied several rooms, Investigation revealed that 
the’ demonstration was spon- 
sored by several members of 
parliament who will probably 
he excluded from the — two 
parties as a result, : 


Party offiginis appealed to the 
Government and after a two-hour 
conference with the Minister of 
the Interior, Admiral Suetsuyn, 
police were instructed to evict 
the demonstrators, It is feared in political cireles 
Y that this merely marks ‘the — be- 
RIGHTS INSISTED ON ginning of a general development 
and that untess the parties take 
up a definite position on the Gen- 
eral Mobilisation Law, which — is 
how being debated in’ the Dict, 
the movement will grow.—Trans- 
Ocean.

AERIAL’ WARFARE grows more terrifying, and the demand for the 


censation of attacks on civilians becomes more clamant. 


These, anti- 


nircraft men ure Kome of the defenders of Madrid. 


fo December, Japanese alr- 
craft sank dhe United States 
ship Panay in the Yangtse, and 
bombed the British warship Lady- 
bird and the other vessels which 
were in her charge. 

That unprovoked aggression is 
already costing the people of the 
Unjted States £160,000,000 for a 
new naval programme. It has 
threatened every British interest 
in the East. 

Yet there are fowr chances out 
of five that the Japanese aircraft 
which committed the assault were 
driven by petrol sold to chem for 
profit by the oil companies of 
Great Britain and the United 
States. ‘ 

Four-fifths of all the oil by 
which the war is carried on reach 
Sapan from British and American 
sources. That is the most 
portant single fact in world 
politics to-day, But-it is a fact 
which the friends of peace have 
apparently forgotten. It is high 
ime that they gave it their atten- 


tion. 


* *” 


ago there were 
acute controversies in Britain 
about “propellant” powders, 
“Cordite’ was a word by which 
political careers might be mado 
vr broken. 

Tu-day, Oil is the most import- 
ant armament “propellant.” Cor- 
dite still hos its uses; it “propels” 
an armour-piercing shell a ton in 
weight .to targets 20 miles away 
across the sca. But ofl propels 

‘sonal bombs to targets hundreds 

» miles away. The bombs may 
welgh a ton, or even more; hun- 
dred-weighc for hundred-weight, 
as the unhappy inhabitants of 
Barcelona have discovered, they 
have ten times the explosive 
power of naval shells. 

Nor is of] only o more efficient 
cordite. Jt has become the main- 
spring of the whole military 
machine.. It propels aircraft, 
tanks, artillery, the lorries which 
bring up food, ammunition, and 
other field requirements to the 
troops. It propels surface war- 
ships, submurines, army trans- 
port vessels, the limousines of the 
General Staffs, the ambulances 
which take the broken cannon- 
fodder to be patched up again. 

. Without oil, the mechanism of 
modern armament would become 
un impgtent

them 96.4 per cent. of the total 
annual oil output of ‘the’ world, 
They are, in order of importance, 
the United States, the U.S.S.K., 
Venezuela, Rumania, Persia, 
Dutch East Indies, Mexico, Iraq, 
and the British Empire. 

Three things should be observ- 
ed about this List. First, no 
Power among them is generally 
regarded as likely to be guilty « 
aggression, Some people dis- 
trust the Government of Moscow; 
but in recent times that Govern- 
ment has done everything within 
its power to show its will for 
peace, . 

Second, all these Powers, with 
one exception, are members, and 
loyal members, of the League. 
The one exception is the United 


States, and we may be very cer-’ 


tain that America would not help 
an agsresgor against the League. 

Even if its “Neutrality Act” 
were put in operation, it would 
support, und not break, a League 
embargo. 

Third, there are only nine of 
these large-scale — oil-producing 
Powers. With so small a num- 
ber, an embargo should be easy 
to establish and easy to control 
When it has been set up. 

ad nT had 

This is not theory only. Those 
who followed closely what hap- 
pened in the autumn of 1935 
know that before the Hoare-Laval 
agreement _ had bedevilled ‘the 
situation, a little courageous 
leadership would have secured a 
general oil embargo against Jtaly. 

The League Committee of Oil 
Experts, by their masterly report, 
and Signor Mussolini, by his 
panic-stricken threats, both made 


it crystal clear that an oil em- 
_ burgo would have ended his bar- 


barous wur. 

He might have ‘been able to 
carry through his first campaign; 
he might possibly have lasted 
through the following rainy sea- 
son; but the second campaign 
could never even have been ‘be- 
gun. : 

An oi] embargo would not be 
less effective against Japan to- 
day. It is the one certuin way 10 
end her aggression in China, to 
bring down her military gang, 
and to put a Liberal Civilian Gov- 


gumbrance to troops onguged in 


war, 3

and humiliating. defeat. With

ber, Japan, according to the best

No General Staff will ever fight

It is objected that, if we stop 
the of} which Japan is now im- 
porting, sho will “go and take 
from the Dutch East Indies all 
that she requires.” Let us 
examine that proposition. 

At present, Japan draws her oil 
supplies from .the following 
sources: US.A, 75 per cent.; 
Dutch East Indies, 12 per cent.; 
British Borneo, 6 per vent.; others, 
8 per cent. (This calculation fs 
based on her Imports from Janu- 
ary, 1936, till June, 1936.) 

If Japan conquered the Dutch 
East Indies, and cook every gallon 
of oil which they produce, . she 
nujrht get enough to carry on. 
But only if she could get the whole 
Dutch supply, and could do so 


without delay. 


+ * > 


Could she do so without delay? 
Everyone knows that the Dutch 
Government has a plan for “pas- 
sive defence”; that is to say, for 
putting their oil-wells out of 
netion. ‘This would mean that 
the Japanese could get no oil from 
those wells for a long period of 
time. 

Even if the wells were not des- 
troyed, could Japan rely on bring- 
ing oil safely from the Dutch 
East Indies to their naval bases, 
3,000 miles away? Only if the 
members ‘of the League decided 
to let her do so. : 

Among them, the member 
States could send to Singapore— 
apart from everything they need 
in Europe—perhaps, 100 sub- 
marines and 1,000 aircraft, apart 
from surface-craft. Is it to be 
believed that the Japaneso Gen- 
eral Staff, with a major and un- 
decided war still on their hands, 
avith the Russian Army waiting 
on their northern flank, with in- 
creasing discontent at home, with 
the growing disapproval of Ger- 
many and the growing hostility of 
mankind at large, would take the 
uppalling risk which on aggression 
on the Dutch East Indies would 
involve?

THE. HONG KONG SUNDAY HERALD, FEBRUARY 20, 1938 


SIGNIFICANCE OF GERMAN 
ARMY CHANGES: PART. 


LICY 


Berlin, Yesterday. 


THE nature and significance of the sweeping 

changes made by Herr Hitler on February 4 

in the command of Germany’s armed forces, 

are examined and elucidated by Major von 

Wedel, attached to the Supreme Command, 

in the current number of the magazine “Die 
Wehrmacht” published yesterday. ~ 


Major 


von Wedel begins with a reference to the 


general trend apparent to-day in all parts 
of the world to bring about greater concen- 
tration in command and direction of the 
forees of national defence. 


He deseribes the state of af- 

fairs with regard to control of the 
army prevailing in the parliamen- 
tary Germany of the . post-War 
period. 2 ‘ 
‘ The authoritarian state, he in- 
sists, cannot tolerate army leader- 
ship devised to conform with, par- 
liamentary ‘government, 

Herr Hitler had done the only 
possible thing when he cume 
into power. Most notable Innova- 
tion was creation of the post of 
supreme command to which all 
the defence services were made 
subordinate, in other words, the 
Defence Minister became at the 
snme time commander-in-chief of 
the combined defence forces. 

Sinee the death of President 
von Hindenburg in August, 1934, 
Herr Hitler has been supreme 
commander-in-chief of the German 
defence forces. 


POLITICAL ACTIVITIES 
Latterly, however, the position 


of things within the Ministry of 
Defence had undergone a change 


in so far as the ofhce staff of the 
Ministry of Defence, which before 
the Nazi regime was engaged for 
the most part in activities of a 
political nature, had been convert- 
vd into the Defence Minister's 
staff of command “with leader- 
ship tasks.” 

Nevertheless the office of the 
Defence Ministry still remained 


and in Sir :Hubert’s |.

Ministry of Defence, 

This has now been changed as 
the result of development of cer- 
tain organisational functions of 
the former Defence Ministry. 


DIRECT CONTROL 


The supreme command of the 
armed forces has now been creat- 
ed to function as the command- 
ing authority of the entire armed 
forces, and placed under the direct 
control of Herr Hitler. 

It will by responsible for unify- 
ing and consvlidating the coun- 
try's defences, 

It is quite obvious, declares Ma- 
jor von Wedel, that the entire 
armed, forces of Germany have 
thus been brought into closer per- 
sonal contact with the Fuchrer, 
who has thus testified as to the 
confidence he reposes in the Ger- 
man urmy. : 


POWER AND POLICY 


Moreover, by creating u secret 
Cabinet, he has intimated - that 
Hikewise in| paramount questions 
of German foreign policy, he con- 
cedes to the German army the role 
which as the State’s instrument 
‘of power, belongs to it by right 
when it comes to tuking decisions 
in the question of foreign policy.

44 FOUN Leaning has given you 
something to_,think about” 
sald a prominent local business 


man when we met on Monday 
evening. He added “You won't find 


it easy to reply to his letter in 


the “Sunday Herald.” My sugges- * 


tion that John Leaning had made 
no reference to the main issuc of 
the controversy between the 
preacher and myself, but had 
jumped into the arena in order to 
advocate his own pet political 
system (Marxism) and that there- 
fore no reply was demanded, pro- 
duced tho crisp retort “You're 
unable to reply!” 


All of my good resolutions to 
leave John Leaning alone were 
shaltered by that challenge. The 
caustic comments of a !earned col- 
league who, umongst other general 
reproofs, said that an engineer 
ought not to discuss socin] or 
theological problems in public. 
and he hoped that such a mistake 
would never be repeated, had made 
me resolve not to irritate him 
again—in the immediate future, 
at leant, But the taunt of the 
man of commerce was more effec- 
tive than the exasperated criti- 
cism of a colleague, The sensitive 
cloistered bookworm, who dis- 
likes any disturbance of 
the academic calm of his 
existence, is distracted when 
he is compelled to consider social 
problems, Ife cannot escape from 
conversation concerning — affajrs 
which the leaders of public opin- 
jon say are of vital importance to 
humanity. It seems to me that the 
scholar has n responsibility as a 
citizen; and that a study of our 
Jucal problems is a duty imposed 
on every citizen. How can the 
schclar sit silently ia his study 
when he reads statements. about 
Hong Kong, or even the world’s 
troubles, which he believes are 
false? It is preferable to listen to 
jeers at the effort to defend social 
evolution, as opposed to Bolshevi- 
kism, rather than experience the 
uneasiness Which results from ao 
failure to face the music. In spite 
of all the anxiety to limit the con- 
troversy to the original istues, 
the attempt will be made to an- 
swer John Leaning, partly because 
my friend in business—the prac- 
tieal man in the stream of life— 
snid that the task was not casy, 
but mainly because John Leaning 
presented his case in such an ad- 
mirable manner, that he may mis- 
lead the unwary, 


The Origin of 
The Dispute 


It in only right to remind read- 
ers of this journal that the ori- 
ginal ‘contribution which roused 
my indignation, viz the Revd. Mac- 
Leon's article “Putting Hong 
Kong on the Spot” did not men- 
tion Marx. Indeed, there are sen- 
tences in that article which beem 
to suggest that the Rovd, MacLean 
does not ontircly approve of Marx- 
ism. He says that he strongly ob- 
jects to domination by any in- 
dividual. He writes of such a por- 
son ne follows “He decides what 
his life's purpose is for everyman, 
Ho chonges the standards for all 
men, the life-standard, the wage- 
standard every standard, and he 
does that becauso he thinks ho is 
a superior creature, and knows!” 
Surely that exactly defines Marx 
and Lenin his practicat disciple 
who worked in Russia, It is a sum- 
mary of my own objection to 
Marxism. 


John Leaning, and the Scots 
preacher, have both ignored the 
libol concerning “the depravity of 
the condition Into which the ma- 
jority of Europeans and middlo 
class Chinnse in Hong Kong have 
fallen," which was a_ sentence 
that created my indignation. As a 
member of the “Middle Class” in 
Hong Kong (labelled “depraved") 
it was impoasible: to allow that 
statement to be circulated without 
an emphatic denial of its truth. 
In addition to thut falsc state- 
ment, the Scots preacher made an 
attack on scicnco; ho blamed 
scientists for our provent- duy so- 
efal difficultics, That also fs false, 
and in China, particularly, whore 
the appalling poverty can only be 
reduced by tho application of 
acientifio principles, demanded the 


categorical denial which 


given, 


was 


Two False Ideas 


Those were the two false ideas 
that were included in the original 
-contribution, and which the pre- 
nent writer bluntly and promptly 
anid were false. Nothing that has 
since appeared in these columns 
has justified the original state- 
ments made by the Scots preacher 
on those two subjects. Other 
issues have been raised, as is in- 
evitable in any controversy. John 
Leaning has seized upon one of 


PROFESSOR 
CAL 
MIDDLETON— 
SMITH 
VENTURES. 

~ AREPLY 


these issues in order to advocate 
Marxism. He has, says my com- 
mercial friend, given me “some- 
thing to think about;” the more I 
think about it the less do I dike 
Marxism. 


Evolution And 
Revolution 


There Is a very great difference 
in the literary style of John Lean- 
ing und that of the ‘Revd. Mac- 
Lean. The former has very de- 
finite ideas, which he makes clear 
and explains with restraint. Al- 
though he supports his thesis with 
arguments that are, for the most 
part, a5 unemotional as a pro- 
position in Euclid, he does make 
us realise that in addition to a 
depth of thought revealed by his 
letter, he also in common with 
many who object to Marxism has 
human sympathy for those in un- 
fortunate economic circumstances, 
His views therefore demand res- 
pectful attention even if they can- 
not be ‘accepted. Many of us who 
would wish to ald in’social and 
economic reforms, cannot agree 
that Marxism, is a practical re- 
medy for social difficulties in Bri- 
tain, or in any country where per- 
xonal liberty is a fundamental 
right. 


Defines The Ideal 


In the last sentence of his let- 
ter John Leaning defines the ideal 
of many thoughtful people who 
have followed recent events in 
Russia with horror and indigna- 
tion. That sentence reads as fol- 
Jows:—"Substitute co-operation 
und social consciousness for ex~ 
ploitation and competition os the 
motive forces of society, and you 
substitute love for hate, humanity 
for inhumanity, in man’s relations 
with his neighbours.” But there 
will always be competition unless 
all men are endowed with equal 
brains and equal strength. 

* The class war, which is dr in- 


tegral—perhaps the dominant— 
feature of Marxism as practiced 


ain Russia, has instilled, not love 


but hate, into the veins of civili- 
sation. Since the Bolshevik regime 
seized power in Russia, it has 
been notablo for man’s in- 
humanity to man. The recent 
“purges” do not encourage us to 
‘believe that humanity has been 
substituted for inhumanity. Hate 
has been emphatically in evidence. 
The terriblé casualty lists result- 
ing from persecution prove that 
to. be a fuet.." It Is tho practical 
evidence of porsecution in Russla, 
and in the Fascist countrics, that 
entitles the lendors of those two 
social systema to: bo labelled 
“blood brothers und: brothers in 
blood.” : 


Will of The People 


It Is not John Leaning’s ultl- 
mate ideal of society that can bo 


criticised; it is the method of at- 
taining it, an demonstrated in 
Russia, that has horrified many 
pedple who ure anxious for social 
improvements, The one outatand- 
ing fact that experience in -en- 
winecring work teaches one is 
that there is a very great differ- 
ence between theory and practice. 
Many Inventions and plans that 
were perfect in theory have fail- 
ed in practice, often because of 
the human element involved in the 
acheme. At the point where know- 
ledge is to be applied in action, in 
social affairs, there in u highly 
variable und incalculable factor. 
That factor is the Will’ of 
the People. The candid stud- 
ent of politics must realise 
that his system of idens resta up- 
on a foundation of assumptions 
that are, of necessity, generalised 
and abstracc; and that, therefore, 
they discount the wilfulness and 
uncertainty of the Immediate si- 
tuation. 


The General Strike 


The will of the people in Rusala 
was, in the crisis when Lenin ap- 
praised swiftly this clement of 
uncertainty in public opinion, very 
different to the will of the people 
in Britain, when their personal 
liberty was threatened by those 
who organised the General Strike. 
That preliminary skirmish on be- 
half of Marxism in England ‘was 
defeated, not because the majority 
of people in England believed tho 
present social system to be per- 
feci, but because they were deter: 
mined to preserve their personal 
Hberty. They have improved sn- 
cial conditions by peaceful evolu 
tion, but they will not accept ty- 
ranny imposed by the proletariat. 
Revolution was inevitable in Rus- 
sia because so many people, in- 
cluding the soldiers, had no food, 
Napoleon beceme a dictator be- 
cause there was neither bread nor 
cake for the hungry in Paris, The 
practical Stalin sits on the ba- 
yonets of the Red Army but the 
theoretical Trocsky is is exile, 


In a national crisis in 
Britain, during the Greut 


War, when Lord Lansdowne and 
others publicly advocated an ear- 
ly peace with Germany, Lloyd 
George relied upon his curious 
capacity for  self-identification 
with the moods of others, and his 
sense of realities; he refused to 
consider peace terms which the 
majority of the British people dis- 
liked. The genius of British states- 
men has been often shown by the 
art of practical decision in a 
crisis. They have made many mis- 
tukes, but they have usually 
known when to strike und when 
to give way; in great emergencics 
they have not waited for analy- 
tical judgments, but have 
d¢iermined, almost by intuition, 
which of several ends to pursue. 


British Temperament 


If Bolshevism has 
succeeded in Russia, it has 
due to the character of the people 
who were unable to organise ro- 
sistance to torrible persecution 
The temperament of the British 
people is entirely different; their 
outstanding characteristic {5 re 
volt against domination. Dean 
Inge writes as follows:—-“Since 
the days of the Saxons and Vik- 
ings, the English tempor has been 
in ordinary times refractory to 
discipline and impatient of ros- 
traint,-each man feeling himself 
fit to rule’, ‘It was the English 
colonists in North America who 
rebelled against the stupid endea- 
vour to aubject them to taxation 
which ‘they believed was unjust. 
Every British cabinet, since that 
date, has profited by that historl 
dofence of liberty, The social 
evolution that has since taken 
place in Great Britain may in duo 
course, produce ‘basic commun- 


partlaliy 


jam; but {f so, it will como only]. 


with the consent of the majority 
of the people nnd it will not be 
Imposed upon them, as was the 
endenvour of Trotsky and his as- 
sociates. Thore will bo no bloody 
civil war and persecution. There 
will be pepceful evolution; that 
hna been taking placo rapidly this 
contury, so. that the social cond!- 
tions of ‘the working class havo 
immensoly improved. The im- 
pravemonts will continuc.

ateps that have been taken since

wide mensure of agreement which

uanisation of the cotton industry 
had received in Lancashire.

basia of the definition of the traden 
tto be affected and were in the

Marx And John 


(Continued from Page 16) 


‘Dean Inge 

John Leaning’s opinion of Marx 
is very different to that of Dean 
Inge, who writes as follows:—"It 
is strange that the human raco, 
which owes to one born in Pales- 
tine the highest and purest re- 
veladon of the path which leads 
to health, peace, and ‘blessedness, 
should be indebted to another 
Jew for a religion of hatred, 
cruelty, and misery. Karl Marx 
started as a violent revolutionist”. 
Let us now quote Hearnshaw on 
Marx who says “He was a man 
embittered by persecution, enrag- 
ed by antagonism, soured by ad- 
versity, exasperated by sufforing. 
His inspiring and dominant pas- 
sion was the passion of hate. It 
was hate that gonded him to his 
enormous literary Inbours; it was 
hate that determined his sclection 
and rejection of historical facts 
for his distorted-description of in- 
dustrial England; it was hate 
that fixed his economic principles, 
that twisted all his arguments, 
that vitinted all his conclusions. 
Dasa Kapital is a work of dogmatic 
religion of repulsion, the Koran 
of the class war”. 

“In hardly amy part of his doc- 
trines was he original”, says Ham- 
macher, a sympathetic critic; “he 
borrowed especially from  Sis- 
mondi”, Dean Inge adds “His 
own contribution’ was the vitriolic 
hatred which appealed to the dis- 
contented ‘and disinherited every- 
where. After'the failure of 1848 
he was driven from Gormany, and 
devoted himself to studying the 
utopian theorists of the past, the 
result being the book above men- 
tioned, which H. G. Wellb. justly 
describes as “a moment of pre- 
tentious pedantry”. - He and his 
friend Engels drow thelr dedur- 
tions primarily from the reports 
of English commissions into the 
conditions of the factory system. 
Dos Kapital. is mainly based on a 


' 

misreading of circumatances pe- 
culiar to England. The centre of 
the system was the prediction 
that under capitalism wealth was 
certain to be concen‘rated in few- 
er and fewer hands, wiping out 
the middle class and reducing the 
working class to the “depths of 
poverty. In two generations ut 
most, the beggars were to dispos- 
sess the millionaires, which would 
be very ensy, since the rich would 
be a mere handful. No forecast 
was ever more witerly belied by 
the event. “The whole’ struc~ 
ture”, says Sombach, himsclf a 
socialist, “has collapsed an silent- 
ly ag the Campanile at Venice”. 
But the catchwords ‘of Marx are 
still repeated, and apparently be- 
lieved. Marx is the saviour-god 
or inspired prophet of Boluhevism. 
Hin gospel of hate has deluged 
Russia with blood”. 


Humanist Side 
of Culture 


There are no political princt- 
ples, in theory, that would: ho 
equally successful in practice for 
n nation of heroes and saints, 4 
community of ascetics and swin- 
ders. Our ability to obtain use- 
ful guidance from theoretical 
aludies in polities and sociology in 
limited by the part which fecting 
prejudices plays in human con- 
duct. The humanist side of cul- 
ture, until revent timer, was fos- 
tered on caste lines, with a mark- 
ed bias in “avour of the wealthy. 
Science hat worked in an opposite 
direction. -It is now Impossible: to 
cunfine seiencdfic knowledge to a 
gmail group,. 18 was astronomy In 
ancient days. Soclalised in 
method, international in scope, 
impersonal in animus, the sciences 
have been building up the possi- 
bility of a secfal system in which 
poverty, and many of the other 
itly that have bedevilled mankind, 
could be eliminated. It is not 
science thay jas delayed the full 
‘utilluation of Nature’s resources 
for the. benefit of all mankind,

Leani 

but ignorance and prejudices and 
n general failure on the part of 
the rulers in all countries to over- 
come old national hatreds and to 
work together in harmony. Wealth 
is nov the only cause of hatred. 
In spite of the advance of science 
the Hindu and the Moslem fly at 


each others throats in a quarrel 
that was originated by a cow! 


Final Answer 

For seven years the writer 
worked with his hands in o large 
engineering factory. His “mates” 
on varioua jobs were’ often un- 
akilled; men, In those days, efri- 
ing about thirty shillings o week. 
They varied in character, as in- 
decd, do the men in a regiment, 
or the students in.a University. 
The more intelligent and  indus- 
trious obtained promotion. My 
objections to Marxism include a 
dislike for any system that re- 
wards the competent and the in- 
competent in the same style. 

Of course, John Leaning will 
not agree with much that has been 
written above. It has not been 
put together lightly; it must ‘be 
my final answer to. his challenge. 
It Is impossible to continuc this 
controversy, but it is only fair 
thut the objections to Marxism 
should be enumerated. The writ- 
er is convinced that the practical 
common sense of the Anglo-Saxon 
people will enable them to con- 
tinue to improve socin! conditions 
in all parts of the British Empire. 
As for China—it is sclence, not 
communism that will conquer po- 
verty.' in ‘that country. Tho de- 
mocratic countries may be in dan- 
ger from attack by Fascist or 
Bolshovik nrmics. They need 
not fear revolutton. Tho redistri- 


bution of wealth’ and . economic]. 


power ensures that, in Britain at 
Jenst, no one starves. And it is 
hunger ‘that causes o rovolution— 
or 'a determination to overthrow 
the oppressor who. denies iiborty 
to those who value personal freo- 
dom.

SILKYLIGHT WINS 


__ THE HONG KONG SUNDAY HERALD, FEBRUARY 20, 1938 


“MAIDEN STAKES. IN EFFORTLESS MANNER | 


Considered To Be Good Bet For To-morrow’s Derby 


MOONLIGHT VIEW 
IMPRESSES IN 
TRIAL PLATE 


KEEN RIVALRY BETWEEN | 


JOCKEYS PRODUCES 
TWO DEAD-HEATS 


ESPITE the overcast skies and consequent cold snap, there 

was a very satisfactory attendance at the opening day of 
the Hong Kong Jockey Club's Annual Race Meeting at the Valley 
yesterday, and the racing served up was fully appreciated by 
the large crowd. 

Dividends were unusually low, suggesting that ruil fans dur- 
ing early training kept nothing from their friends. ‘The highest 
sum paid by the parimutuel was $41.80 for Katinka’s (Mr. Ruuph). 
win in the Bendigo Stakes, ‘The “Daily Double,” however, pro- 
duced & much more gratifying return, at least for one person, 
paying oul $8,037.00 for the combination of Criffel (which had 
85 backers) and Desert Star. The sole backer is believed to have 
been the owner of Desert Star. : 

Cash Sweeps were not as high as expected, but this was prob- 
ably due to the faet that most people were buying tickets in the 
mammoth Derby sweep, which is now nearing the $310,000 mark. 
‘The highest first prize $1,00 cash sweep was $2,094.40, while the 
$2 sweep was $2,870.00,

Scotland Win Rugby 
| International 


Weak English Team Well 7 
Beaten 25 To 8 


pete ed trounced England by two gonls, four tries and 
} penalty gon] (26 points) to a goal and a penalty goal (8 
points) in the Final of the local International Rugby Tournament 
on the Sookunpoo ground yesterday afternoon. 

Weakness in the pack and at the base of the scrum seriously 
handicapped the English team, their backs being rarely given an 
opportunity of breaking away, although Walsham, Pratt and 
Luscombe tried hard, individually, to break through the strong 
Scottish defence. : 


Wrst re saarens 


Scotland made one change from 
Jast week, Henderson being moved 
from full-back to scrum-half, 
while Cessford was brought In at 
full-back, Bonnar was the absen- 
tee, being on the sick Hat. Eng- 
land had several changes. Woods 
was an absentee from the scrum- 
half position, which he creditably 
filled Inst Saturday when Bailey 
was injured, and Webster, a form- 
er Navy “cup,” made one of his 
rare appenrances to fill the 
vacancy. Dufty, full-back, and Sgt. 


Burston (forward) both played 

in the “A” fifteen match previous 

to the big match of the afternoon, 

but both stood up well to their ' 
task, 

Close bunching by the Scottish 
forwards and excellent work in 
the lineouts by Taylor, Oliphant 
and Miller laid the. foundation for 
most of the Scottish tries, while 
Crawford, Dunnett and Watson. 
shone in the loose. 

Cessford was the pick of the 
two backs, his handling of the ball

‘Older and better than ever 


_ Never wan your White Horse as good or as old asit is 
toeday. Years ago, exceptionally heavy stocks of the 
finest whiskies were laid down to anticipate salea which 
the quality would justify. These stocks are now fully 
maturcd. The reaule is thac WHITE HORSE js now 
older and be:ter than ever before, excellent as it was 
in 1932 and preceding years. 

This advance in quality and age will be maintained. 
White Horse is now the equivalent of a fine liqueur.

Challengers 
Tennis 


Although the entries for the Colony 
Lawn ‘Tennis Championships do not 
hy any means constitute a record, 
they van nevertholoss be considered 
very sntinfaetory, 4 

Both events, the Singles and the 
Doubles, will be enlivened this year 
hy the appearanco of Tsui Wai-pui, 
who was forced to withdraw during 
the Jaut tournament in order to Join 
the Chinese Davis Cup team in 
Kurope, 

. Since his return he has woi the 
Colony Hardcourt Singles and Dou- 
bles titles, and bts form oh that oc- 
enasion is certain to make him a 
firm favourite for the  Grasscourt 
Championships. He how entered the 
doubles with hin brother, Taul Yan- 
pui with whom he wak suceessful in 
1936 after beating the Rumjahn 
cousins: 

The evergreen Rumjahns, “S.A.” 
and “HD, have again entered and 
are Hkely ta be as prominently fea- 
tured as in former years in both 
events, while other players in the 
veteran stage who will be on view 
include, M. W. Lo, a former holder 
and orobably the oldest competitor, 
B.C. Fincher, W. oC. Hung and 
W, Leonard! 

A promising youngster, George 
Chon, who crented a great impression, 
in his debut last season, will again 
be competing and {tx likely to give a 
good account of himself after a sen- 
son in the First Division of the Len- 
gue, 

Prominent doubles combination, in 
addition to the Tsuis and the Rum- 
Jahns, are B.C. Fincher and W. C, 
Itung, FF. H. Kwok and SW, Linng, 
A, V. Remedios and A. V. Gosano, J, 
W. Leonard and G, Chon and Luk 
Ding-cheonge and Wong  Shiu-wing, 
while M. K. and M, W. Lo are also 
likely to make their presence felt. 

The tournnment will start carly 
next month,

é i: » fF : i _ CHAMPIONSHIP 


THE Central British Association Ladies regis- 


tered their first win this season in the Caer 
Clark Cup hockey competition when they unex- 
pectedly beat Hong Kong Ladies at King’s Park 


by a goal scored late in 
White. 
The -C.B.A. suecess 


the first half by Mrs 


has given “Y” Ladies 


custody of the trophy for the second gear in 
succession. The “Y” can now afford to lose their 


C.B.A. showed good form, 
their forwards displaying bet- 
ter combination than their op- 
ponents and having more of the 
game. In the second period 
they were continually on the 
attack, Mrs, Lunson being call- 
ed on numerous occasions to 
defend her charge, while the 
few oceasions on which Hong 
Kong Ladies became dangerous 
they were met with stout resis- 
tance from Miss Whitley and 
Miss P. Woolley. 


Mrs. White, centre-forward, 
wus the brains of a lively C.B.A. 
attack. She initatiated most of the 
moves and was always a danger 
wh on in possession. Her passes to 
the wingers were well placed, 
though Miss D. Hunt, on the left 
wing, spoiled several promising 
moves in the firat half by being 
offside. ; 


Tho (R.A. half-back line was 
also prominent, Miss B, Parsons, 
in the pivotal position, worked 
hard and was 2 stumbling block 
to the visitors throughout the 
game, and, though beaten on 
several occasions, she held Miss 
Marsh well in check and was al- 
ways up with the forwards. Her 
wing-halves, Miss Clewer and 
Miss I, Woolley, were also ood.

GOALLESS DRAW 


For scrappy play, St. Andrew's 
Junior Ladies and Reereio Ladies 
were well matched yesterday when 
they clashed in the Brawn Cup hoc- 
key series for the runners-up position 
at Shumshuipo. 

From start to finish neither side 
were seen at their best, and the 
match finishod up with both sides 
failing to register a score, 

St. Andrew’n full backs, Misr E. 
Chang and, Miss J. Brondbridge, dis- 
played more spirit than the rest of 
their colleagues, but failed to come 
up to.previous form.

often taking the ball at their feet 
for 40 or 60 yards. 

England's task became so hope- 
less in the second half that Wata- 
ham was forced to punt down the 
centre in the hope that some of 
his forwards would follow up and 
carry the ball-ovor, but they were 
rarely, if ever, up. : 

Grieve was always a danger 
when in possession, but the rest of 
the backs rarely came into the 
picture. Gudgeon’s handling was 
poor, while Stewart was never 
given a chance, the majority of the 
passes which came his way being 
very bad. Penchey, on the other 
wing, was a marked man and could 
not make much progress, 

Scotland took the initiative in 
the acoring when Stewart crossed 
the English line wide of the posts, 
Watson failing to add the goal 
points. Soon after Miller was 
penalised for offside and Walsham 
kicked a penalty goal to level the 
scoring 

After 2 series of midfield mauls, 
England carried play into Scot- 
land’s '25, where Luscombe gain- 
ed possession and managed to 
scramble over the Scottish lino 
wide of the posts for Walsham 
to add the goal points with an ex- 
cellent kick. 

England’s success was short- 
lived, however, as they were penal- 
ised for offside in thelr own '25, 
Webster being the culprit, and 
Oliphant had no difficulty in kick- 
ing n penalty goal. 


SCOTLAND EXERT PRESSURE 


Scotland were now having the 
better of the game and, after a 
serich of thrusts had been re- 
pulacd, Henderson picked: up a 
loose ball and dashed over for a 
good try between the posts for 
Oliphant to add the goal points 
and thus yive Scotland an inter- 
val lead of 11 points to 8. 

England felt away badly in the 
second half and could do nothing 
right, in spite of several openings, 
and Scotland went further ahead 
when Taylor took a bouncing ball 
in his astride to score between the 
posts for Oliphant to add the goal 
points. 

Further disaster befell England 
svon after as Luscombe pulled a 
thigh muscle and had to leave the 
field for good. Following this set- 
back Scotland went ahead and 
further tries came from Crawford 
(2) and Duncan, all of which were 
not converted.

Short Story. - 
SETTING: 


HE January night was pitch 

black, made all the blacker 

by the sheeting rain which aeemed 

to wash away the very rays of 

light from the headlamps on Bar- 
ton’s car. : 

The country rond along which 
the car was ploughing was nar- 
row, bounded by tall, trees and 
sernb hedges. It was Cull of pol- 
holes, partly flooded. 

He was lost. He had taken the 
wrong turning oat a crosa-roads 
miles behind. When he hod dis- 
covered thin he had returned to 
the cross-roada, but had again 
tuken a wrong turning. Or was 
it the same cross-ronds? He could 
not be sure. 

Barton drew che car to a halt 
and studied a map. He could not 
locate these roads on the map. 

He drove on 
slowly, trying his 
best to avoid the 
treacherous holes 
in the rond, He 
realised that tho 
car was begin- 
ning to climb 
upwards — grad- 
ually. 7 

He ‘had gone about half a mile 
when he saw a light. [t was 4 


Night In 


mere pin-point at first. He press- 
ed the accelerator and the rar? 
shot ahead. He was impetuous 
now with the joy of relief. The 
light yrew larger, then vanished. 

He went forward once more, 
sick at heart. Then he reached 
the mouth of a lane. He realised 
now ihat the light) vame © from 
nome spot along the lane, He 
turned the car towards the light. 

ee 

The Jane twisted into the shape 
of n clumsy question mark, Bar 
ton knew then why the light dis- 
appeared so mysteriously. It was 
coming from a spot on the out- 
ward curve of the question mark 
and was visible only when he was 
some distance from the mouth of 
the lane. 
Jean house standing in a little 
yard. He switched off the car en 
vine and clambered out into an 
kle-deep mud. He tried to open 
the little iron gate lending inte 
the yard, but it was padlocked. 

He rattled the gate and shouted 
again and again. No answer 
came to his cries. He climbed 
over the gate and crossed — the 
yard. He thudded on the door, 
The sounds of his beating fist 
boomed in the hall behind — the 
door as though he were strikius 
at an enormous drum. 

He walked ‘round the house, 
peering in at each windgw. Aa 
he preased his face close to one 
window in un attempt to pierce 
the darkness within he was star- 
tled by the movement of a white 
shadow near to the glass. His 


blood chilled as he realised that cruel twist to it, 


this shadow was u human face. 
For a moment he thought that the 
face was poised in the air, unsup- 
ported. Then he saw movement 
below it, and gradually, the out- 
linca of a body were revealed to 
him. 

He hammered on the window. 
The fate and body seemed io fade 
into the darkness of the 
room beyond. He was almost 
certain now that he had be- 
held an apparition. lic ran round 
the housc, intending to get ‘away 
from this strang, ‘building as 
quickly as possible. “But when he 
reached the front of the house he 
saw 2 man standing in the door- 
way. He paused in his rush and 
blurted out, “Can you tell me how 
{ can reach. ‘* But the voice of 


the man cut him short, “You're 


lost—ceh?” 

“Yeu,” ‘said Barton, “I've been 
trying-to get over the hills.” 

“Don’t let it worry you,” the 
man interrupted. “We're all Jost. 
That should comfort you. 
Come in, sir,” said the man, a tri- 
fle imperiously it seemed. “You're 
wet and cold. Come in-and xct. 
some heat into your boncs.”

Inferences During The Play 


T can be said that scarcely a 

card is laid down during tho 
play of the hand without convey- 
ing mu message. A well-known 
example is the lead of the fourth- 
highest , of a suit’ again a No 
Trumps contract. If the two In led, 
then it Is.clear that the leader 
holds no more than four of his 
long suit. At the same time,’ in 


inodern play, it is quite common to 


lead, say, the fifth, or even the 
Hixth-highest, of a Jong suit 
against No Trumps—for instance, 
the 3 or the 2, holding K 108532 
—with a view to deceiving the de- 
clurer. 


The “Eleven Rule,” which is 

lend of the 
fourth-highest, is a good example 
of how inferences can be made 
during the play, It ix a moot point 
whether the unorthodox lead of 
the 4 or 2) mentioned above in 
worth while, It may deceive the 


bused oon the 


declarer; almost inevitably it will 


deceive the partner, Ag a result, , 
rwhen he secures the Jead himself, 


he may switch to another suit, 
thinking the opening 2 or 8 in- 
diculed a four-eard, or at most 


five-card, holding. 


Further up the scale of yalues 
the lead of a King originally, purt- 
bid the suit, in- 
dicates: possession of either the 
Ace or Queen, or both, Yet, here 
again, among “foxy” players, the 
King may 


ner not having 


be a paychic lead, a 
singleton, or even a shot in the 


dark, without vither Ace or Queen, 


In short, what must be under- 
stood is that, in Contract Bridge 
as phiyed by good exponents to- 
day, it is very difficult sometimes 
to make the right inference from 
a specitic card, We have progressed 
4 long distunce—some aver it has 
been a retrogression—from «-the 
old Whist days when a false card 
was considered the equivalent of 
w black untruth, . 


The following deal, based on a 
famous hand analysed by the Jate 
I: Bergson in the Gays of Auction 
Bridge, serves to show how the 
making of intelligent inferences 
during the play ‘will help the de- 
elarer with a diMeult contract.

The bidding was direct, al-

West made the orthodox open- 


best suit—the 3 of Spades! When

lable, including the 2 of Spades,

that Weat held only four Spades 
originally. East put up the Queen,

Next the Queen of Diamonds

fell, This looked like a singleton, 
but to make sure South led a small 
Diamond to Dummy’s Ace. On that 
trick West was forced to make a 
diseard—the 2 of Clubs: 

N“\ 


Now some more inferences could 
be made by South. Weat’s, lead 
originally was from a four-enrd

4 singleton Diamond, and appar-

would surely have preferred to 
open a four-eenrd suit headed by 


an King to a four-card suit headed. 


by a Tack, 


saving made these inferences, 
South felt save to-finesse against 
Kast for beth of the | missing 
Kings, And, as may ‘be seen from 
the complete deal given above, 
South's reasoning enable him to 


make all 13 tricks, where another

WAZIRISTAN 


London, Yesterday. . 

Total British casualtics between 
January and September last year 
in the fighting in Waziristan, 
amounted to 183 killed and 149 
wounded, while the tribesmen’s 
losses included 700 dead and 350 
seriously wounded, according to a 
report issued in the supplement of 


the London Gazette. 

Gencral Sir Robert Caasels, Gen- 
eral OMicer Commanding in Egypt, 
declares in the report: “TE canno' 
apeak too highly of the R.A.F.+ 
which played such n great part in 
suppression of the risings'on the 
North-West Frontier. : 

“During day and night opera- 
tions they covered over 1,000,000 
milos in 11.000 hours dying.” 

The G.O.C. adds that detaits of 
the newly formed Indian air force 
nico took part In the operations.— 
Reuter. : 


BY WAFDISTS IN 
ALEXANDRIA 


Alexandria, Yesterday. 

Over 60 are reported to have 
been injured in the course of 
grave disturbances here to-day 
in connection with the ciection 
campaign. 

The disorders occurred after 
a demonstration of adherents of 
the Wafd (Nationalist) Party, 
led by the ex-Premier, Nahas 
Pasha, in person. 

Police on'y succeeded after 
much difficulty in restoring 
order. — Trans/Ocean.

Hankow, Yesterday. 


“The morale of the Chinese forces is good,” stated 


a foreign traveller 


who arrived in Hankow 


to-day from Hsuchow, having travelled by 
train on the Lunghai Railway and the Pei- 
ping-Hankow Railway via Chengchow. 


The situation, he reveals 


, is not so serious at Kai- 


feng as had heen reported. 


“Only 300 Japanese 


troops at present are at 


Fengchiu, near,the north bank of the Yellow 


River, 18 miles north of Kaifeng, 
river itself is strongly 


while the 
defended, both on the 


northern and southern banks, at Kaifeng by: 
troops under General Shang Cheng,” the for- 
mer Governor of Honan Province. 


Following the retreat of 
troops under General Wan Fu- 
‘in'and General Sung Cheh-yuan 
from Changtch to Sinhsiang, 
the traveller declared. that the 
arrival of Central Government 
troops on the Peiping-Hankow 
Raitway front had brought a 
considerable improvement in the 
situation’ there. . 


. WARM PRAISE

ed the Shanghai front five times, 
and he was prepared to say that 
the morale of the Chinese troops 
on the Tientsin-Pukow Railway 
front was “much higher than that 
shown in the Shanghai fighting.” 

General Li ‘Faung-jen, comman- 
der-in-chief of the Chinese forces 
on the T.P.R. front, is winning 
the admiration und support of 
both troops and -public. 

The foreigner sald: “When one 
enters Hsuchow one sees posters 
everywhere bearing the inscrip- 
tion ‘Resist to the last inch- of 
scorched ground.’ 


‘HWAI RIVER HERO

former Mayor of Ticntsin was

casualties but inflicted at least 
8,000 cusualtics on the Japanese 
forces. : 

“General Yu Hsueh-chung’s 
former Manchurian ‘troops also 
fought well at Linhwal.” 

Scene of the main battle on 
the T.P.R. front hns now shifted 
to Hwalyuan, Tingyuan and 
Feneyuan, where crack Central 
Government divisions command- 
ed by General Tang En-po (de- 
fender of Nankow Pass) assist- 
ed by artillery units, are Jaun- 

. ching a vigorous attack on the 
Japanese flank, “thus neccssl- 
tating withdrawal of the Jap- 
anese troobs north of the Hwai 
River to the Hwafyuan aren.”

Chen Chien, commander-in-chicf 

of the Chinese forces on’ the Pel-

not perturbed by devolonmenis 
there, and only a small. force of 

Tananese is participating at pre-

PROGRESS IN 
SHANSI 


Peiping, Yesterday. 

Japanese troops operating in 
Shansi province have advanced 
to Pingjao, about 100 kilometres

GREEK SHIP 
SEIZED AND 


Shanghai, Yesterday. 

_A Greek steamer, the “Ano- 
vathi,” is reported to have been 
seized by a Japanese destroyer 
while journcying from Wenchow 
to Shanghai. 

She was towed to Shanghai, 
where she was released upon re- 
presentations to Vice-Admiral 
Hasegawa, the Japancse comman- 
der-in-chief, by the Greck consular 
authorities. ; 

Reason for the Japancse action 
is unknown.—Reuter. 


Bid To Cut 
Off China 
From Soviet 


Hankow, Yesterday. 
While the Japanese forces are 
likely to continue their drive on 
the Lunghni Railway front, the 
muin body of Japanese, 80 well- 
informed Chinese circles here 
believe, will be used for the 
forthcoming offensive aimed at 
securing occupation of China's 
north-western provinces, therc- 
by cutting off communications 
between China and Russia. 
Chinese observers believe that 
tho Japanese will use fresh divi- 
sions from Manchuria to’ launch 
the drive into Southern Shanai 
and them swing towards Shensi, 
Kanau and Ninghsia and scize the 
“back door” to China.—Reuter. 


NEW G.S.0. 1 IN 
‘MALAYA 


London, Yesterday. 

Lt.-Col. E. G. Miles (Royal 
Berkshire Regiment), formerly 
of the Shanghai Defence Force, 
has been promoted Colonel and 
appointed G.S.0.1., Malaya. 

Lt. Col. Miles has commanded 
the First Battalion of the Berk- 
shires since 1936, and is 47 
years of age. 

He joined the King’s Own 
Scottish Borderers in 1911 and 
served in the Great War, when 
he way awarded the D.S.O. and 


M.C. 

In 1923 he was G.S.O. 3 at 
the War Office, and from 1927 
to 1928 was Brigade Major, 
Shanghai Defence Force. 

He was appointed G.S.O. 2 in 
1930 and held this rank at the 
War Office until 1933, 

In 1934 he went to the Im- 
perial Defence College, and was 
promoted Lieutenant-Colonel in 
1936.—Reuter. :
